(1-(1-\exp\left(-8\xi\cdot D\left(\tau\Big\|p\right)+o(\xi)\right))^{\frac{n}{4\xi}})=\left(1-(1-\frac{x}{m})^{m}\right),

(329)

Using
(1-\frac{x}{m})^{m}\leq 1-\frac{x}{2}
for
0\leq x\leq 1
, we obtain the first term; while for
x>1
,
(1-(1-\frac{x}{m})^{m})\geq(1-(1-\frac{1}{m})^{m})=\Omega(1)
, we obtain the second term.

Substituting
\xi
with
\log(n/\varepsilon)
, we get that

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\geq\min\left\{n\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{n}\right)^{8D(\frac{1-\frac{k}{n}}{2}\|p)+o(1)},\Omega(1)\right\}.\end{split}

(330)

This equation shows that, for the block-encoding scheme, given the noise strength
p
, a trade-off relation between the encoding rate
\frac{k}{n}
and the Choi error exists. If one fixes an encoding rate, the Choi error will have a lower bound, and vice versa. Below, we prove that there exists a regime of the strength parameter
p
where the double-layer blocked encoding scheme achieves both a higher encoding rate and a lower Choi error. Moreover, there exist regimes of noise strength and encoding rate such that the double-layer blocked encoding scheme requires a lower depth to make the Choi error decay.

Recall Corollary
3
, the Choi error for the double-layer blocked encoding scheme is:

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathfrak{C}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq(2^{-n(1-\log(1+3p)-\frac{k}{n})}+\frac{4\varepsilon^{1-\frac{k}{n}}n^{\frac{k}{n}}}{\log(n/\varepsilon)})^{\frac{1}{4}}.

(331)

The main contributions are
2^{-\frac{n}{4}(1-\log(1+3p)-\frac{k}{n})}
and
(\frac{4\varepsilon^{1-\frac{k}{n}}n^{\frac{k}{n}}}{\log(n/\varepsilon)})^{\frac{1}{4}}
. In the following, we discuss the case of
1-\log(1+3p)-\frac{k}{n}>0
. For simplicity, we set
r=\frac{k}{n}
and
\varepsilon=n^{-\frac{\alpha+r}{1-r}}
where
r
and
\alpha
and both positive constants;
p
is also a constant. Then,
\frac{n}{\varepsilon}=n^{\frac{1+\alpha}{1-r}}
. The block size parameter
\xi=\log\frac{n}{\varepsilon}=\frac{1+\alpha}{1-r}\log n
. The upper bound of the Choi error for the double-layer blocked encoding scheme is simplified to:

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathfrak{C}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq(2^{-n(1-\log(1+3p)-r)}+\frac{4(1-r)}{(1+\alpha)n^{\alpha}\log n})^{\frac{1}{4}}=O(n^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}}).

(332)

The lower bound of the Choi error for the block-encoding scheme is simplified to:

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\geq\min\left\{n^{1-\frac{8(1+\alpha)}{1-r}D(\frac{1-r}{2}\|p)+o(1)}=n^{c_{\mathrm{block}}},\Omega(1)\right\}.

(333)

In the case that
0<p<1/3,0<r<1,\alpha>0
and
1-\log(1+3p)-r>0
, the Choi error of the double-layer blocked encoding scheme always decays. The decay scale is approximately
O(n^{-\frac{\alpha}{4}})
where we ignore
\log n=n^{o(1)}
. On the other hand, the exponential coefficient within Eq. (
333
) is

c_{\mathrm{block}}=1-\frac{8(1+\alpha)}{1-r}D(\frac{1-r}{2}\|p)+o(1).

(334)

Given noise strength
p
, under the constraint of
r<1-\log(1+3p)
and
\alpha>0
,
c_{\mathrm{block}}
can be greater than 0. For instance,

p=0.25,r=0.2,\alpha=0.1\Rightarrow c_{\mathrm{block}}\approx 0.14\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{B}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\geq\min\{n^{0.14},\Omega(1)\}=\Omega(1).

(335)

That is, given the noise strength
p=0.25
and encoding rate
r=0.1
, we obtain that the double-layer blocked encoding scheme can have an error decay
n^{-0.025}
, and the block-encoding scheme cannot make the Choi error decay. In this case,
\xi=1.375\log n
. As mentioned in the main text, the depths for the two encoding schemes are the same after fixing
\xi
. This manifests that for
p=0.25
and
r=0.1
, the double-layer blocked encoding scheme requires a lower depth to make the Choi error vanish. On the other hand, this implies that at the same circuit depth, the double-layer blocked encoding scheme can achieve a higher encoding rate.

More concretely, one can find parameter regimes such that
c_{\mathrm{block}}>0
. In this case, the double-layer blocked encoding scheme can correct errors, but the block-encoding scheme cannot. One can also find parameter regimes such that
c_{\mathrm{block}}>-\frac{\alpha}{4}
. In this case, the double-layer blocked encoding scheme always outperforms the block-encoding scheme by manifesting a faster error decay. We plot the regimes that
c_{\mathrm{block}}>0
and
c_{\mathrm{block}}>-\frac{\alpha}{4}
in Figs.
5
and
5
, respectively.

Figure 5:
(a) Parameter regime where
c_{\mathrm{block}}>0
. In this case, the double-layer blocked encoding scheme can make the Choi error decay, but the block-encoding scheme cannot. (b) Parameter regime where
c_{\mathrm{block}}>-\frac{\alpha}{4}
. In this case, the double-layer blocked encoding scheme outperforms the block-encoding scheme.

Below, we show the proof of Theorem
6
.

Proof.

We first observe that the choice of subset
Q
is irrelevant when considering the expectation over the random stabilizer encoding scheme.

Lemma 10
.

For any fixed subsets
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subseteq[n]
with
|Q_{1}|=|Q_{2}|=t
,

\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{C,Q_{1}}=\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{C,Q_{2}},

(336)

where
\epsilon_{C,Q_{i}}
is the Choi error for encoding unitary
C
and erasure errors on
Q_{i}
.

Proof.

Because the SWAP operation is Clifford, there exists a Clifford
S_{12}
that swaps
Q_{1}
to
Q_{2}
. Hence

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{C,Q_{2}}&=\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{S_{12}C,Q_{1}}\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{C,Q_{1}}.\end{split}

(337)

∎

Fix the subsets
Q_{1}=[t]
,
Q_{2}=\{t+1,\dots,2t\}
, and
Q_{3}=[n]\backslash(Q_{1}\cup Q_{2})
so that
Q_{3}
contains the remaining
n-2t
physical qubits. We now prove the following.

Lemma 11
.

For any unitary encoding
C
,

\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\epsilon_{Q_{1}}+\epsilon_{Q_{2}}\bigr)\geq\frac{2t-(n-k)-e^{-1}}{8k}.

(338)

Proof.

Let
\epsilon_{1}\coloneqq\epsilon_{Q_{1}}
and
\epsilon_{2}\coloneqq\epsilon_{Q_{2}}
. Also, recall that we denote
L
,
R
, and
S
as the logical, reference, and physical systems, respectively. The initial input state is the
\hat{\phi}_{LR}
, and after encoding and noise, the state is denoted as
\hat{\phi}_{LR}
.

By definition of
\epsilon_{Q}
, there exists a recovery map
\mathcal{D}_{Q_{2}Q_{3}\!\rightarrow L}
such that

\sigma=\mathcal{D}\!\bigl(\hat{\phi}_{LR}\bigr),\qquad P\bigl(\sigma_{LR},\hat{\phi}_{LR}\bigr)=\epsilon_{1}.

(339)

Because the purified distance bounds the trace distance,

d_{\tr}\bigl(\sigma_{LR},\hat{\phi}_{LR}\bigr)\leq\epsilon_{1}.

(340)

Applying the Fannes–Audenaert inequality
[
65
]
with subsystem size
|LR|=2k
gives

S(\sigma_{LR})\leq S(\hat{\phi}_{LR})+2\epsilon_{1}(2k)+\tfrac{1}{e}=4k\epsilon_{1}+\tfrac{1}{e}\coloneqq\delta_{1}.

(341)

Therefore,

I_{\sigma}(Q_{1}:R)\leq I_{\sigma}(Q_{1}:LR)\leq 2S(\sigma_{LR})\leq 2\delta_{1}.

(342)

Here,
I_{\sigma}(A:B)
is the mutual information between subsystems
A
and
B
for state
\sigma
. The first inequality comes from the data processing inequality for mutual information, and the second uses
I_{\sigma}(A:B)\leq 2S(\sigma_{B})
for arbitrary
\sigma
.
Let
\rho\coloneqq\hat{\phi}_{LR}
. Because the recovery map
\mathcal{D}
acts only on
Q_{2}Q_{3}
, the registers
Q_{1}
and
R
are untouched, so

I_{\rho}(Q_{1}:R)=I_{\sigma}(Q_{1}:R)\leq 2\delta_{1}

(343)

Expanding mutual information and using that
\rho
is pure, we obtain

S(\rho_{Q_{1}})+S(\rho_{R})\leq S(\rho_{Q_{1}R})+2\delta_{1}=S(\rho_{Q_{2}Q_{3}})+2\delta_{1}\leq S(\rho_{Q_{2}})+S(\rho_{Q_{3}})+2\delta_{1},

(344)

where the last step uses subadditivity. Repeating the same argument for
Q_{2}
gives

S(\rho_{Q_{2}})+S(\rho_{R})\leq S(\rho_{Q_{2}R})+2\delta_{2}=S(\rho_{Q_{1}Q_{3}})+2\delta_{2}\leq S(\rho_{Q_{1}})+S(\rho_{Q_{3}})+2\delta_{2}.

(345)

Adding the two inequalities yields

2S(\rho_{R})\leq 2S(\rho_{Q_{3}})+2(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}).

(346)

With
S(\rho_{R})=k
and
S(\rho_{Q_{3}})\leq|Q_{3}|=n-2t
, we obtain

\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}\geq 2t-(n-k).

(347)

Substituting (
341
), we have

\frac{1}{2}(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})\geq\frac{2t-(n-k)-e^{-1}}{8k}.

(348)

∎

Based on Lemma
10
and Lemma
11
, we prove Theorem
6
as follows:

\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{Q_{1}}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{Q_{1}}+\mathbb{E}_{C}\epsilon_{Q_{2}})=\mathbb{E}_{C}\frac{\epsilon_{Q_{1}}+\epsilon_{Q_{2}}}{2}\geq\frac{2t-(n-k)-e^{-1}}{8k}.

(349)

∎

Appendix C
Analysis for 1D brickwork circuits

In this part, we prove that the decoupling can be achieved by 1D log-depth brickwork circuits. We start with introducing preliminaries, which discuss how to evaluate
\tr O_{2}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(O_{1})
by transforming it into a stochastic process. Then, we present the main results of the decoupling theorem for 1D brickwork circuits and prove Theorem
2
. Finally, we apply this decoupling theorem to quantum error correction and show that there exists a constant threshold for encoding schemes with such circuits against local noise. Note that here we prove the result for a generic local dimension
q
.

C.1
Random quantum circuits to stochastic processes

Here, we show how to evaluate
\tr O_{2}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(O_{1})
by transforming it into a stochastic process for certain observables
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, which lays a foundation for further analyzing the decoupling error. The evaluation is equivalent to evaluating a statistical-mechanical partition function. Most of the analysis is similar to that in Ref.
[
32
]
. Nonetheless, in Ref.
[
32
]
,
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
take specific forms of
\outerproduct{1^n}{1^n}^{\otimes 2}
, which makes the boundary condition in the statistical-mechanical mapping easier. For general operators
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
, the boundary condition is more complicated, and a more refined analysis is necessary.

We first evaluate
\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(O_{1})
. By definition,

\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(O_{1})=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathcal{E}}U^{\otimes 2}O_{1}U^{\dagger\otimes 2},

(350)

where

U=U^{(s)}_{A^{(s)}}U^{(s-1)}_{A^{(s-1)}}\cdots U^{(1)}_{A^{(1)}}.

(351)

We would like to evaluate terms like
\mathbb{E}_{U}U^{\otimes k}OU^{\dagger\otimes k}
. Also,
U^{(i)}
is a Haar-random gate of dimension
q^{2}
. Inserting random single-qudit operations before all two-qudit gates does not change the expectation. Thus, we further consider the random unitary operation of the following form.

U=U^{(s)}_{A^{(s)}}U^{(s-1)}_{A^{(s-1)}}\cdots U^{(1)}_{A^{(1)}}\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)}_{\{i\}}.

(352)

Here,
U^{(-i)}_{\{i\}}
is a random single-qudit operation with dimension
q\times q
acting on qudit
i
. It is worth noting that when analyzing 1D brickwork circuits, we also insert a random single-qudit operation
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)}_{\{i\}}
before the random two-qudit unitary operations within Eq. (
2
).

We continue the analysis by evaluating the actions of each part within
U
. The action of
\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)}_{\{i\}}
on
O_{1}
can be evaluated by Eq. (
51
). We obtain that

\mathbb{E}_{U^{(-i)}}(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)\otimes 2}_{\{i\}})O_{1}(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)\dagger\otimes 2}_{\{i\}})=\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\sum_{\vec{\gamma}\in\{I,S\}^{n}}\tr(O_1 \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma_j})\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}.

(353)

Here,
\gamma_{j}
is the
j
-th component of
\vec{\gamma}
and equals identity operation
\mathbb{I}
or SWAP operation
F
. The term
g_{\gamma_{j}}=\mathbb{I}-q^{-1}F
if
\gamma_{j}=\mathbb{I}
, and
g_{\gamma_{j}}=F-q^{-1}\mathbb{I}
if
\gamma_{j}=F
. The vector
\gamma
is called a configuration, corresponding to the spin configuration is the statistical-mechanical mapping. For simplicity, we denote
c_{\vec{\gamma}}(O_{1})=\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\tr(O_1 \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma_j})
and use
\vec{\gamma}
to represent
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\gamma_{j}
in the above equation. That is,

\mathbb{E}_{U^{(-i)}}(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)\otimes 2}_{\{i\}})O_{1}(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}U^{(-i)\dagger\otimes 2}_{\{i\}})=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}c_{\vec{\gamma}}(O_{1})\vec{\gamma}.

(354)

The next step is evaluating the action of a random two-qudit gate
U^{(i)}_{A^{(i)}}
on
\vec{\gamma}
, which was previously studied in Ref.
[
32
]
. Since
A^{(i)}
only contains two qudits, it suffices to analyze how
U^{(i)}_{A^{(i)}}
acts on
\gamma
within support
A^{(i)}
. Denote
M^{(i)}(O)=\mathbb{E}_{U^{(i)}}U^{(i)\otimes 2}_{A^{(i)}}OU^{(i)\dagger\otimes 2}_{A^{(i)}}
, we have that

M^{(i)}(O)=\tr_{A^{(i)}}(O\frac{\mathbb{I}_{A^{(}i)}-q^{-2}F_{A^{(}i)}}{q^{4}-1})\mathbb{I}_{A^{(i)}}+\tr_{A^{(i)}}(O\frac{F_{A^{(}i)}-q^{-2}\mathbb{I}_{A^{(}i)}}{q^{4}-1})F_{A^{(i)}}.

(355)

Particularly, we consider that
O
equals
\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I}
,
\mathbb{I}\otimes F
,
F\otimes\mathbb{I}
, and
F\otimes F
within support
A^{(i)}
. We have that

\displaystyle M^{(i)}(O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes(\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I})_{A^{(i)}})

\displaystyle=O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes(\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I})_{A^{(i)}},

(356)

\displaystyle M^{(i)}(O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes(F\otimes F)_{A^{(i)}})

\displaystyle=O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes(F\otimes F)_{A^{(i)}},

(357)

\displaystyle M^{(i)}(O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes(\mathbb{I}\otimes F)_{A^{(i)}})

\displaystyle=O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes\left(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1}(\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I})_{A^{(i)}}+\frac{q}{q^{2}+1}(F\otimes F)_{A^{(i)}}\right),

(358)

\displaystyle M^{(i)}(O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes(F\otimes\mathbb{I})_{A^{(i)}})

\displaystyle=O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}\otimes\left(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1}(\mathbb{I}\otimes\mathbb{I})_{A^{(i)}}+\frac{q}{q^{2}+1}(F\otimes F)_{A^{(i)}}\right).

(359)

Here,
O_{\overline{A^{(i)}}}
only has support outside
A^{(i)}
. From the above equation, we obtain that if
O\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}
, then
M^{(i)}(O)\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}
. Particularly,
M^{(i)}
is a linear transformation over
\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}
, and we use
M^{(i)}_{\vec{\nu}\vec{\gamma}}
to denote the matrix element such that

M^{(i)}(\vec{\gamma})=\sum_{\vec{\nu}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}M^{(i)}_{\vec{\nu}\vec{\gamma}}\vec{\nu}.

(360)

Suppose
A^{(i)}=\{a_{i},b_{i}\}
, the matrix element
M^{(i)}_{\vec{\nu}\vec{\gamma}}
satisfies

M^{(i)}_{\vec{\nu}\vec{\gamma}}=\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} 1,\end{aligned}\quad&\text{if }\gamma_{a_{i}}=\gamma_{b_{i}}\text{ and }\vec{\gamma}=\vec{\nu},\\
\begin{aligned} \frac{q}{q^{2}+1},\end{aligned}\quad&\text{if }\gamma_{a_{i}}\neq\gamma_{b_{i}},\nu_{a_{i}}=\nu_{b_{i}},\text{and }\gamma_{c}=\nu_{c},\forall c\in[n]\backslash\{a_{i},b_{i}\},\\
\begin{aligned} 0,\end{aligned}\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(361)

It can be seen that each action of
M^{(i)}
maintains the configuration
\vec{\gamma}
with weight
1
, or flips one term in
\vec{\gamma}
with weight
\frac{q}{q^{2}+1}
. Thus,
\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(O_{1})
can be expressed as follows.

\begin{split}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{E}}(O_{1})&=M^{(s)}\cdots M^{(2)}M^{(1)}(\sum_{\vec{\gamma}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}c_{\vec{\gamma}}(O_{1})\vec{\gamma})\\
&=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}c_{\vec{\gamma}}(O_{1})M^{(s)}\cdots M^{(2)}M^{(1)}(\vec{\gamma})\\
&=\sum_{\Gamma=(\vec{\gamma}^{(0)},\cdots,\vec{\gamma}^{(s)})\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n\times(s+1)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}(O_{1})\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}\prod_{i=1}^{s}M^{(i)}_{\vec{\gamma}^{(i)}\vec{\gamma}^{(i-1)}}.\end{split}

(362)

Here,
\Gamma=(\vec{\gamma}^{(0)},\cdots,\vec{\gamma}^{(s)})
is called a trajectory, which is a sequence of
s+1
configurations. We also use
\Gamma_{i}
to denote its
i
-th component. Here,
\Gamma_{i}=\vec{\gamma}^{(i-1)}
. Furthermore,

\begin{split}\tr(O_2\Phi^2_{\mathcal{E}} (O_1))&=\sum_{\Gamma}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}(O_{1})\tr( O_2\Vec{\gamma}^{(s)})\\
&=\sum_{\Gamma}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2}),\end{split}

(363)

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{s}M^{(i)}_{\vec{\gamma}^{(i)}\vec{\gamma}^{(i-1)}},

(364)

\displaystyle c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\tr(O_1 \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^{(0)}_j})\tr(O_2 \bigotimes_{j=1}^n \gamma^{(s)}_j).

(365)

When
O_{1}=O_{2}=\outerproduct{1^n}{1^n}^{\otimes 2}
,
c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2})=\frac{1}{(q+1)^{n}}
, which is the case studied in Ref.
[
32
]
. By mapping
\{\mathbb{I},F\}
to
\{+1,-1\}
, a configuration
\vec{\gamma}
is a spin variable, and the trajectory
\Gamma
is a two-dimensional Ising spin configuration. Correspondingly,
\tr(O_2\Phi^2_{\mathcal{E}} (O_1))
is mapped into a partition function of a classical Ising spin model, which is a weighted summation of different trajectories. For each trajectory
\Gamma
, the total weight depends on two terms. The first one is
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
, where the value depends on the whole trajectory. The second term is
c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2})
, and the value only depends on the initial and ending configurations of the trajectory. Note that
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
is always non-negative since the matrix element
M^{(i)}_{\vec{\gamma}^{(i)}\vec{\gamma}^{(i-1)}}
is always non-negative.

For further analysis, we write

\tr(O_2\Phi^2_{\mathcal{E}} (O_1))=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2})\sum_{\Gamma\backslash\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma).

(366)

Here
\sum_{\Gamma\backslash\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}
takes the summation of trajectories that start with
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
and end with
\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}
.
In the following, we denote
Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}=\sum_{\Gamma\backslash\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
and evaluate this term to analyze
\tr(O_2\Phi^2_{\mathcal{E}} (O_1))
. We start with analyzing
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
with the method of random walk.

Given a fixed set of supports
\{A^{(1)},A^{(2)},\cdots,A^{(s)}\}
, the action of
\prod_{i=1}^{s}M^{(i)}
on a configuration
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
results in a length-
(s+1)
random walk
\Gamma
over
\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}
. The initial location of the random walk is a fixed configuration,
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
, and at time step
t
, the location transitions from
\vec{\gamma}^{(t-1)}
to
\vec{\gamma}^{(t)}
. The configuration after step
1
may not be fixed. Based on Eq. (
361
), the transition must follow the following rule: the configuration is unchanged from
\vec{\gamma}^{(t-1)}
to
\vec{\gamma}^{(t)}
if the operators of
\vec{\gamma}^{(t-1)}
within support
A^{(t)}
are
\mathbb{I}\,\mathbb{I}
and
FF
; there is an identity operation flipped into a SWAP operation or a SWAP operation flipped into an identity operation if the operators of
\vec{\gamma}^{(t-1)}
within support
A^{(t)}
are
\mathbb{I}F
and
F\mathbb{I}
, and the probability of each case of flip is
\frac{1}{2}
. If the configuration is unchanged, the contributed weight of this transition to
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
is
1
, and if there is a flip, the contributed weight is
\frac{q}{q^{2}+1}
. As a result,
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
depends on how many flips within trajectory
\Gamma
. That is,

\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}},

(367)

where
\#F_{\Gamma}
is the number of flips within
\Gamma
. It can also be written as

\begin{split}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)&=(\frac{1}{2})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}(\frac{2q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}\\
&=\Pr(\Gamma)(\frac{2q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}},\end{split}

(368)

where
\Pr(\Gamma)=(\frac{1}{2})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}
is the probability of trajectory
\Gamma
in this random walk.

To calculate
Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}
, we need to count all trajectories satisfying the transition rule and require the start and ending configurations to be
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
and
\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}
, respectively. Then, we obtain that

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}=\sum_{\Gamma}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{s+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}.

(369)

Here,
\mathbf{1}_{A}
is the indicator function, which equals
1
if
A
is satisfied and 0 otherwise. Using Eq. (
368
), we have that

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}=\mathbb{E}_{\Gamma}(\frac{2q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{s+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}.

(370)

Note that the weight of a particular walk or trajectory in the above random walk relates to the number of flips during the walk, which brings challenges in evaluating its value. To tackle this issue, we turn the above unbiased random walk into a biased random walk. In the original unbiased random walk, the flip has an equal probability from
\mathbb{I}
to
F
and from
F
to
\mathbb{I}
. In the new biased random walk, we set the probability from
\mathbb{I}
to
F
as
\frac{1}{q^{2}+1}
, and the probability from
F
to
\mathbb{I}
as
\frac{q^{2}}{q^{2}+1}
. Accordingly, we can modify the form of the weight of a trajectory into the following.

\begin{split}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=&(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}\\
=&(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F^{\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F}_{\Gamma}}(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F^{F\rightarrow\mathbb{I}}_{\Gamma}}\\
=&(\frac{1}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F^{\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F}_{\Gamma}}(\frac{q^{2}}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F^{F\rightarrow\mathbb{I}}_{\Gamma}}q^{\#F^{\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F}_{\Gamma}-\#F^{F\rightarrow\mathbb{I}}_{\Gamma}}\\
=&\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma)q^{\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}},\end{split}

(371)

where
\#F^{\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F}_{\Gamma}
is the number of flips from
\mathbb{I}
to
F
within
\Gamma
,
\#F^{F\rightarrow\mathbb{I}}_{\Gamma}
is the number of flips from
F
to
\mathbb{I}
within
\Gamma
, and
\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma)=(\frac{1}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F^{\mathbb{I}\rightarrow F}_{\Gamma}}(\frac{q^{2}}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F^{F\rightarrow\mathbb{I}}_{\Gamma}}
is the probability of trajectory
\Gamma
in the biased random walk, and
\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(i)}}
is the number of SWAP operators in
\vec{\gamma}^{(i)}
. Then,

\begin{split}Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}&=\sum_{\Gamma}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{s+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}\\
&=\sum_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma)q^{\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{s+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}\\
&=q^{\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\Gamma}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{s+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}\\
&=q^{\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}).\end{split}

(372)

Here,
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\Gamma}
represents the expectation over biased random walk, and
\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow\vec{\gamma}^{(s)})
represents the probability of this biased random walk transitioning from
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
to
\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}
.

For an infinite-size circuit or
s\rightarrow\infty
, the final configuration of the random walk can only take
\mathbb{I}^{n}
and
F^{n}
since they are the fixed points of the biased random walk. It was found that in this case the probability of
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
transitioning into
\mathbb{I}^{n}
or
F^{n}
only depends on the weight of
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
or
\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}

[
32
]
. The concrete values are given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow\mathbb{I}^{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-q^{-2n+2\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}}{1-q^{-2n}}

(373)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow F^{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{-2n+2\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}-q^{-2n}}{1-q^{-2n}}.

(374)

We can use the above results of the infinite-size circuit to derive the
\tr O_{2}\Phi^{2}_{Haar}(O_{1})
for the Haar-random ensemble. This can be used to check the soundness of the derivation.

From Eq. (
51
), we have that

\begin{split}\tr O_{2}\Phi^{2}_{Haar}(O_{1})&=\tr O_{2}\left(\tr(O_1 \frac{\id^n-q^{-n}F^n}{q^{2n}-1})\mathbb{I}^{n}+\tr(O_1 \frac{F^n-q^{-n}\id^n}{q^{2n}-1})F^{n}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{q^{2n}-1}(\tr O_{1}\mathbb{I}^{n}\tr O_{2}\mathbb{I}^{n}+\tr O_{1}F^{n}\tr O_{2}F^{n}-q^{-n}\tr O_{1}\mathbb{I}^{n}\tr O_{2}F^{n}-q^{-n}\tr O_{1}F^{n}\tr O_{2}\mathbb{I}^{n}),\end{split}

(375)

where
\mathbb{I}
and
F
are the identity and SWAP operators between two qudits, respectively.

Based on Eqs. (
365
), (
366
), (
372
), (
373
), and (
374
), we have that

\begin{split}\tr O_{2}\Phi^{2}_{Haar}(O_{1})=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2})Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}\\
=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{1},O_{2})q^{\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow\vec{\gamma}^{(s)})\\
=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\mathbb{I}^{n}}(O_{1},O_{2})q^{-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow\mathbb{I}^{n})+\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}F^{n}}(O_{1},O_{2})q^{n-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\widetilde{\Pr}(\Gamma:\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\rightarrow F^{n})\\
=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\mathbb{I}^{n}}(O_{1},O_{2})q^{-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\frac{1-q^{-2n+2\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}}{1-q^{-2n}}+\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}F^{n}}(O_{1},O_{2})q^{n-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\frac{q^{-2n+2\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}-q^{-2n}}{1-q^{-2n}}\\
=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}(\tr(O_1 \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^{(0)}_j})\tr(O_2 \id^n))q^{-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\frac{1-q^{-2n+2\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}}{1-q^{-2n}}\\
&+\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}(\tr(O_1 \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^{(0)}_j})\tr(O_2 F^n))q^{n-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\frac{q^{-2n+2\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}-q^{-2n}}{1-q^{-2n}}\\
=&\frac{1}{q^{2n}-1}\tr(\tr O_1\id^n\tr O_2\id^n + \tr O_1F^n\tr O_2F^n - q^{-n}\tr O_1\id^n\tr O_2F^n - q^{-n}\tr O_1F^n\tr O_2\id^n).\end{split}

(376)

In the final equation, we use the fact that

\begin{split}\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}q^{-\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}g_{\gamma^{(0)}_{j}}&=(1-q^{-2})^{n}\mathbb{I}^{n},\\
\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}q^{\absolutevalue{\Vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}g_{\gamma^{(0)}_{j}}&=(q-q^{-1})^{n}F^{n}.\end{split}

(377)

Thus, our derivation from the infinite-size circuit is in accordance with that from the Haar-random ensemble, verifying the soundness of the derivation. In the next section, we will focus on the 1D log-depth brickwork circuits.

C.2
Decoupling with 1D brickwork circuits and proof of Theorem
2

Below, we give the formal proof of Theorem
2
.

As shown in Appendix
B.1
, since
\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
is a unitary-
1
design, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{U}\|\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\rho_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})-\tau_{E}\otimes\rho_{R}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{U}\tr[\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})\right)^{2}]-\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_E)\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_R)},

(378)

and

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}_{U}\tr[\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})\right)^{2}]=\tr_{S}(\Phi^{2}_{\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}(\tr_{R}(\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}^{\otimes 2}F_{R}))(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{S\rightarrow E}^{\dagger})^{\otimes 2}(F_{E})),\end{split}

(379)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{S\rightarrow E}=\sigma_{E}^{-1/4}\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}\sigma_{E}^{-1/4}
with the corresponding Choi-Jomiołkowski representation
\widetilde{\tau}_{SE}=\sigma_{E}^{-1/4}\tau_{SE}\sigma_{E}^{-1/4}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}=\zeta_{R}^{-1/4}\rho_{SR}\zeta_{R}^{-1/4}
. In the following, we denote
O_{SR}=\tr_{R}(\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}^{\otimes 2}F_{R})
and
O_{SE}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{S\rightarrow E}^{\dagger})^{\otimes 2}(F_{E})
. The following analysis is to give the upper bound of
\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})
.

Note that for 1D brickwork circuits, the random unitary is denoted as

U=\prod_{l=1}^{d}U^{[l]}.

(380)

Thus, instead of defining
\vec{\gamma}^{(i)}
by transforming
\vec{\gamma}^{(i-1)}
with a two-qudit gate
U^{(i)}
, we define
\vec{\gamma}^{i}
by transforming
\vec{\gamma}^{i-1}
with a layer of two-qudit gates
U^{i}
. Particularly, we set
\vec{\gamma}^{0}
equaling
\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}
and define
M^{i}
such that

M^{i}(O)=\mathbb{E}_{U^{i}}U^{i\otimes 2}OU^{i\dagger\otimes 2}.

(381)

Then,

\begin{split}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})&=M^{d}\cdots M^{2}M^{1}(\sum_{\vec{\gamma}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}c_{\vec{\gamma}}(O_{SR})\vec{\gamma})\\
&=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}c_{\vec{\gamma}}(O_{SR})M^{d}\cdots M^{2}M^{1}(\vec{\gamma})\\
&=\sum_{\Gamma=(\vec{\gamma}^{0},\cdots,\vec{\gamma}^{d})\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n\times(d+1)}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}}(O_{SR})\vec{\gamma}^{d}\prod_{i=1}^{d}M^{i}_{\vec{\gamma}^{i}\vec{\gamma}^{i-1}}.\end{split}

(382)

Here,
\Gamma=(\vec{\gamma}^{0},\cdots,\vec{\gamma}^{d})
is called a trajectory, which is a sequence of
d+1
configurations. Note that we view
M^{i}
as a transfer matrix as follows.

M^{i}(\vec{\gamma})=\sum_{\vec{\nu}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}}M^{i}_{\vec{\nu}\vec{\gamma}}\vec{\nu}.

(383)

Correspondingly,

\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})=\sum_{\Gamma}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{SR},O_{SE}),

(384)

where

\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}M^{i}_{\vec{\gamma}^{i}\vec{\gamma}^{i-1}},

(385)

and
c_{\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}\vec{\gamma}^{(s)}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})
is given by Eq. (
365
).

Following the analysis in Appendix
C.1
, we have that

\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}},

(386)

where

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}=\sum_{\Gamma}(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{d+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{d}}.

(387)

In the following, we will use the domain wall method to analyze the above quantity.

C.2.1
Definition of domain wall

Given a configuration
\vec{\gamma}\in\{\mathbb{I},F\}^{n}
, we define the associated set of domain-wall locations as

DW(\vec{\gamma}):=\{e\in\{1,2,\cdots,n\}:\gamma_{e}\neq\gamma_{e+1}\}.

(388)

Define
g^{i}=DW(\vec{\gamma}^{i})
. The number of domain walls within
g^{i}
is denoted as
\absolutevalue{g^i}
. For a trajectory
\Gamma=(\vec{\gamma}^{0},\cdots,\vec{\gamma}^{d})
, we can define its domain-wall trajectory
G(\Gamma)=(g^{0},\cdots,g^{d})
. If
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)\neq 0
, then its associated domain-wall trajectory must satisfy the following rule:
If there is a domain wall at location
e
, and a gate acts on qudits
\{e,e+1\}
, then the domain wall must move from
e
to
e-1
or
e+1
. If there already exists a domain wall at these locations, the two domain walls will annihilate with each other. On the other hand, a pair of domain walls cannot be created from a vacuum. Meanwhile, due to the boundary condition, the number of domain walls must be even.

Based on Eq. (
2
), the
l
-th layer quantum gate
U^{[l]}
only contains two-qudit gates acting on
\{2i-\mod(l,2),2i+1-\mod(l,2)\}
. Thus, the domain walls within
g^{l}
must have opposite parity with respect to
l
. For a specific domain wall within
g^{0}
, it may move or stay still at the first step. Starting with the second step, the domain wall will keep moving and survive until the final step or annihilate with another domain wall during the movement. The number of domain walls cannot increase within a trajectory.

For each trajectory
\Gamma
, its weight,
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=(\frac{q}{q^{2}+1})^{\#F_{\Gamma}}
, is uniquely determined by its associated domain-wall trajectory
G(\Gamma)
. Each movement of a domain wall within
G(\Gamma)
corresponds to a flip. Hence, we denote
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{weight}(G(\Gamma))
.

A domain-wall trajectory
G
can always be decomposed into two disjoint parts. That is, we define
G_{1}=(g^{0}_{1},\cdots,g^{d}_{1})
and
G_{2}=(g^{0}_{2},\cdots,g^{d}_{2})
such that

G=G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}\equiv(g^{0}_{1}\sqcup g^{0}_{2},\cdots,g^{d}_{1}\sqcup g^{d}_{2}),

(389)

where
\sqcup
is the disjoint union. Thus,
g^{l}_{1}\cap g^{l}_{2}=\emptyset
for all
l
. This decomposition satisfies that
\mathrm{weight}(G)=\mathrm{weight}(G_{1})\mathrm{weight}(G_{2})

[
32
]
. This relation is essential in evaluating the bound of
\mathrm{weight}(G)
.

For each trajectory
\Gamma
, we decompose its domain-wall trajectory
G(\Gamma)
into two disjoint domain-wall trajectories,
G(\Gamma)=G_{U}\sqcup G_{0}
, as shown in Fig.
6
. Here,
G_{U}
has a conserved number of domain walls, and the domain walls of
G_{0}
will annihilate before the final step. That is,
g_{U}^{d}=g^{d}
, and
g_{0}^{d}=\emptyset
. This decomposition is unique
[
32
]
. In the following, we separately give the analysis of the upper bound and lower bound for
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
and
Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}
. For further elaboration, we denote
\mathcal{G}_{U}
and
\mathcal{G}_{0}
as the subsets of domain-wall trajectories without annihilation and without a surviving domain wall at the end, respectively. Also, we denote
\mathcal{G}_{U,k}\subset\mathcal{G}_{U}
as the set of domain-wall trajectories always with
k
domain walls. For a 1D periodic circuit structure,
k
must be even to make
\mathcal{G}_{U,k}\neq\emptyset
.

Diagram for the domain-wall trajectory decomposition. The total qubit number is

and the circuit depth is

. The first figure is a trajectory

with corresponding domain-wall trajectory

. The number of domain walls within

does not conserve.

.

C.2.2
Upper bound of
Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}

In this part, we derive the upper bound of
Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}
, presented in the following lemma.

Lemma 12
.

Given the set of trajectories
\Gamma
with initial and final configurations as
\vec{\gamma}^{0}
and
\vec{\gamma}^{d}
, we divide these trajectories into different sets with fixed
G_{U}
,

\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}=\{\Gamma|G(\Gamma)=G_{U}\sqcup G_{0}\}.

(390)

Given the domain-wall trajectory
G_{U}
with initial configuration
g_{U}^{0}=\{e^{0}_{1},e^{0}_{2},\cdots,e^{0}_{2k_{0}-1},e^{0}_{2k_{0}}\}
. Here,
2k_{0}=\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}
is even. Denote
E^{0}_{odd}=\{1,\cdots,e^{0}_{1},e^{0}_{2}+1,\cdots,e^{0}_{3},\cdots,e^{0}_{2k_{0}}+1,\cdots,n\}
and
E^{0}_{even}=[n]\backslash E^{0}_{odd}=\overline{E^{0}_{odd}}
. We also denote
\vec{\gamma}_{A}
as the component of
\vec{\gamma}
within support
A
, and denote
\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}_A = F}
and
\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}_A = \id}
as the number of SWAP and identity operators within
\vec{\gamma}_{A}
, respectively. Then,

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}\leq\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\overline{\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}.

(391)

Here, we denote
\overline{F}=\mathbb{I}
and
\overline{\mathbb{I}}=F
. And
\eta=q/(q^{2}+1)
.

Proof.

Recall that

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}=\sum_{\Gamma}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{d+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{d}}.

(392)

The summation is over all trajectories from
\vec{\gamma}^{0}
to
\vec{\gamma}^{d}
with non-zero weight. Note that
\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{weight}(G(\Gamma))
. We can analyze the domain-wall trajectories of all
\Gamma
for evaluating the weight. Note that
\Gamma
has a fixed ending configuration
\vec{\gamma}^{d}
, implying that
G(\Gamma)
also has a fixed ending point
DW(\vec{\gamma}^{d})
. Following the analysis of the previous subsection, the domain-wall trajectory
G(\Gamma)
can be uniquely decomposed into the disjoint union of two domain-wall trajectories
G_{U}
and
G_{0}
such that
g_{U}^{d}=DW(\vec{\gamma}^{d})
and
g_{0}^{d}=\emptyset
. After the decomposition, we determine and denote
g_{U}^{0}
as the initial point of the domain-wall trajectory
G_{U}
. Note that
G_{U}\in\mathcal{G}_{U,\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}
and
\absolutevalue{g_U^0}=\absolutevalue{g_U^d}=\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}
.

We then divide all trajectories
\Gamma
, equivalently, all
G(\Gamma)
, according to
G_{U}
. Particularly, we define the set of trajectories with fixed
G_{U}
:

\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}=\{\Gamma|G(\Gamma)=G_{U}\sqcup G_{0}\}.

(393)

Given a fixed
\vec{\gamma}^{d}
, the number of distinct
G_{U}
or different sets
\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}
is upper bounded by
2^{d\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}
. For each domain wall
e
inside
DW(\vec{\gamma}^{d})
, moving it forward only has two directions. Moving it back to the first layer only has
2^{d}
choices. Since there is only
\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}
domain walls, moving all of them back to the first layer only has
2^{d\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}
options, which gives the upper bound of the number of distinct
G_{U}
and
\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}
. Then, we have

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}=\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{1}=\vec{\gamma}^{(0)}}\mathbf{1}_{\Gamma_{d+1}=\vec{\gamma}^{d}}.

(394)

Below, we analyze the bound of
\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)
. For each trajectory
\Gamma
, we have
G(\Gamma)=G_{U}\sqcup G_{0}
. Since there is no annihilation of domain walls within
G_{U}
, the number of domain-wall movements is at least
(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}
, implying that

\mathrm{weight}(G_{U})\leq\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}
with
\eta=q/(q^{2}+1)
. Thus,

\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{weight}(G_{U})\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})\leq\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}),

(395)

and

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(\Gamma)\leq\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}).

(396)

The following derivation is to further evaluate the upper bound of
\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})
. Given the domain-wall trajectory
G_{U}
with initial configuration
g_{U}^{0}
and ending configuration
g_{U}^{d}=DW(\vec{\gamma}^{d})
, we write
g_{U}^{i}
as
\{e^{i}_{1},e^{i}_{2},\cdots,e^{i}_{2k_{0}-1},e^{i}_{2k_{0}}\}
. Here,
2k_{0}=\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}
is even. We denote the support
E^{i}_{odd}=\{1,\cdots,e^{i}_{1},e^{i}_{2}+1,\cdots,e^{i}_{3},\cdots,e^{i}_{2k_{0}}+1,\cdots,n\}
and
E^{i}_{even}=[n]\backslash E^{i}_{odd}=\overline{E^{i}_{odd}}
as demonstrated in Fig.
7
. We also denote
\vec{\gamma}_{A}
as the component of
\vec{\gamma}
within support
A
, and denote
\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}_A = F}
and
\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}_A = \id}
as the number of SWAP and identity operators within
\vec{\gamma}_{A}
, respectively. We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 13
.

If
\vec{\gamma}^{d}_{\{1\}}=\mathbb{I}
,

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=F}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\id}};

(397)

if
\vec{\gamma}^{d}_{\{1\}}=F
,

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\id}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=F}}.

(398)

Proof.

For
\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}^{G}_{U}
,
G_{U}
within the decomposition of
\Gamma
is fixed. After fixing
G_{U}
, we can separate
G_{0}
into
\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}
regions.

In step
i
, the
j
-th region is defined as
R_{j}^{i}=\{e^{i}_{j-1}+1,e^{i}_{j-1}+2\cdots,e^{i}_{j}\}
. And we divide
g^{i}_{0}\in G_{0}
into the union of its parts in different regions,
g^{i}_{0}=g^{i}_{0}|_{R_{1}^{i}}+g^{i}_{0}|_{R_{2}^{i}}+\cdots+g^{i}_{0}|_{R_{\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}^{i}}
. In the overall picture, the region
j
is the union of all
R_{j}^{i}
across different steps. Correspondingly, we can decompose
G_{0}
into the disjoint union of trajectories in different regions:
G_{0}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}G_{0}^{j}
where
G_{0}^{j}=(g^{0}_{0}|_{R_{j}^{0}},g^{1}_{0}|_{R_{j}^{1}},\cdots,g^{d}_{0}|_{R_{j}^{d}})
. An illustration of regions is demonstrated in Fig.
8
.

Since
G_{0}=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}G_{0}^{j}
, we have that

\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})=\prod_{j=1}^{\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j}),

(399)

and

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}^{G}_{U}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})=\prod_{j=1}^{\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j}).

(400)

In the following, we will derive the upper bound of
\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j})
. Since the domain walls within
G_{0}
annihilate in the end,
\vec{\gamma}^{d}
must be the tensor product of all identity operations or SWAP operations within a region. Depending on which case holds, we call this region a type-
\mathbb{I}
region or a type-
F
region. If Region
j
is a type-
\mathbb{I}
region, we will show that

\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{R_j^0}=F}}.

(401)

If Region
j
is a type-
F
region, we will show that

\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{R_j^0}=\id}}.

(402)

\mathbb{I}

). An example of a type-
\mathbb{I}

. Given this region, we first extend it to a wider region. Particularly, we define

,

, and
R_{j}=\{e_{j-1}^{\min}+1,e_{j-1}^{\min}+2,\cdots,e_{j}^{\max}\}
. Then for each configuration
\vec{\gamma}^{i}|_{R_{j}^{i}}
, we extend it to
\vec{\gamma^{\prime}}^{i}|_{R_{j}}
by filling the extra components with identity operations. Region
j
is correspondingly extended to Region
j^{\prime}
as shown in Fig.
9
. By extending the region, we make sure that the configurations of different steps have equal length. Also, since we extend the region, the number of valid domain-wall configurations within the new region can only increase. Thus, we have

\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j})\leq\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{\prime j}).

) and (

(405)

Hence,

\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0|_{R_j^0} = F}}.

(406)

\begin{split}\sum_{\Gamma|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{j})&\leq\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{\prime j})\\
&\leq\sum_{\Gamma^{\prime}_{inf},\Gamma^{\prime}|_{\text{Region }j}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0}^{\prime j})\\
&=q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma'}^0|_{\text{Region } j} = \id}}\frac{q^{2\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma'}^0|_{\text{Region } j} = \id}}-q^{-2n}}{1-q^{-2n}}\\
&\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma'}^0|_{R_j^0} = \id}}\\
&=q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0|_{R_j^0} = \id}}.\end{split}

(407)

) and (

) are proved. Combining these two equations with Eq. (

), we obtain that

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}^{G}_{U}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})\leq\prod_{j:\text{ type-}\mathbb{I}}q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0|_{R_j^0} = F}}\prod_{j:\text{ type-}F}q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0|_{R_j^0} = \id}}.

(408)

\vec{\gamma}^{d}_{\{1\}}=\mathbb{I}
, Eq. (

) reduces to

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=F}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\id}}.

(409)

When
\vec{\gamma}^{d}_{\{1\}}=F
, Eq. (
408
) reduces to

\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\mathrm{weight}(G_{0})\leq q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\id}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=F}}.

(410)

Proof is done.

Applying Lemma
13
and Eq. (
396
) to Eq. (
394
), we get

Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}\leq\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\overline{\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}.

(411)

Note that we denote
\overline{F}=\mathbb{I}
and
\overline{\mathbb{I}}=F
.

C.2.3
Proof of Theorem
2

Applying Lemma
12
, we get the upper bound of
\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})
. Recall that

\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})=\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}},

(412)

where

c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})=\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\tr(O_{SR} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^{(0)}_j})\tr(O_{SE} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n \gamma^{(s)}_j).

(413)

Note that
O_{SR}=\tr_{R}(\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}^{\otimes 2}F_{R})
and
O_{SE}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{S\rightarrow E}^{\dagger})^{\otimes 2}(F_{E})
. Below, we utilize the tensor product structure of
\rho_{SR}
,
\rho_{SR}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}
. Then,
\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}
and
O_{SR}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}O_{S_{i}R_{i}}
. Since
\tr(O_{S_iR_i} g_{\id})=\tr(\widetilde{\rho}_{R_i}^2)-q^{-1}\tr(\widetilde{\rho}_{S_iR_i}^2)\geq 0
and
\tr(O_{S_iR_i} g_F)=\tr(\widetilde{\rho}_{S_iR_i}^2)-q^{-1}\tr(\widetilde{\rho}_{R_i}^2)\geq 0
, we have
\tr(O_{SR} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^{(0)}_j})\geq 0
. Meanwhile,
\tr(O_{SE}F_A\otimes\id_{\Bar{A}})=q^{2n}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}\geq 0
. Thus, we will always have
c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})\geq 0
. Then,

\begin{split}\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})Z_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}\\
\leq&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}c_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}(O_{SR},O_{SE})\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\overline{\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}\\
=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}\vec{\gamma}^{d}}\frac{1}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\tr(O_{SR} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^0_j})\tr(O_{SE} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n \gamma^d_j)\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\overline{\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}\\
=&\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\frac{\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}}\tr(O_{SR} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^0_j})\tr(O_{SE} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n \gamma^d_j)q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\overline{\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\vec{\gamma}^d_{\{1\}}}}\\
=&\sum_{DW(\vec{\gamma}^{d}),\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\frac{\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}}{(q^{2}-1)^{n}}\sum_{\vec{\gamma}^{0}}\tr(O_{SR} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n g_{\gamma^0_j})\tr(O_{SE} \bigotimes_{j=1}^n \gamma^d_j)(q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=\id}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=F}}+q^{-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{odd}}=F}-\absolutevalue{\vec{\gamma}^0_{E^0_{even}}=\id}})\\
=&\sum_{DW(\vec{\gamma}^{d}),\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}q^{-2n}(\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{odd}}F_{E^{0}_{even}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{odd}}F_{E^{d}_{even}}+\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{even}}F_{E^{0}_{odd}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{even}}F_{E^{d}_{odd}})\\
=&q^{-2n}(\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}^{n}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}^{n}+\tr O_{SR}F^{n}\tr O_{SE}F^{n})+q^{-2n}\sum_{\absolutevalue{DW(\Vec{\gamma}^{d})}\in\{2,4,\cdots,2\lfloor n/4\rfloor\}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\times\\
&\sum_{\mathcal{S}_{U}^{G}}(\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{odd}}F_{E^{0}_{even}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{odd}}F_{E^{d}_{even}}+\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{even}}F_{E^{0}_{odd}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{even}}F_{E^{d}_{odd}}).\end{split}

(414)

Here,
\mathbb{I}_{A}
and
F_{A}
means identity and SWAP operations on the support
A
The last inequality comes from Eq. (
377
). Note that

\displaystyle q^{-2n}\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}^{n}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}^{n}

\displaystyle=\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{E})\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{R}),

(415)

\displaystyle q^{-2n}\tr O_{SR}F^{n}\tr O_{SE}F^{n}

\displaystyle=\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{SE})\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{SR}).

(416)

Also,

\begin{split}q^{-2n}\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{odd}}F_{E^{0}_{even}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{odd}}F_{E^{d}_{even}}&\leq\max_{E^{0}}(\frac{\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{odd}}F_{E^{0}_{even}}}{\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{odd}}F_{E^{d}_{even}}})\max_{E^{d}}(q^{-2n}\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{odd}}F_{E^{d}_{even}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{odd}}F_{E^{d}_{even}})\\
&\leq\max_{i\in[n]}(\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}},\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}})^{\absolutevalue{E^d_{even}-E^0_{even}}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2})\\
&\leq\max_{i\in[n]}(\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}},\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}})^{d\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}).\\
\end{split}

(417)

Similarly,

\begin{split}q^{-2n}\tr O_{SR}\mathbb{I}_{E^{0}_{even}}F_{E^{0}_{odd}}\tr O_{SE}\mathbb{I}_{E^{d}_{even}}F_{E^{d}_{odd}}\leq\max_{i\in[n]}(\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}},\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}})^{d\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}).\end{split}

(418)

Note that
\absolutevalue{\mathcal{S}_U^G}\leq 2^{d\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}
. Then,

\begin{split}\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})\leq&\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_E)\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_R)+\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{SE})\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{SR})+\sum_{\absolutevalue{DW(\Vec{\gamma}^{d})}\in\{2,4,\cdots,2\lfloor n/4\rfloor\}}\eta^{(d-1)\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\times\\
&(2\rho_{m})^{d\absolutevalue{DW(\vec{\gamma}^d)}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2})\\
\leq&\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_E)\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_R)+\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{SE})\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{SR})+\\
&\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor}\binom{n}{2k_{0}}((2\rho_{m})^{d}\eta^{(d-1)})^{2k_{0}}),\end{split}

(419)

where we denote
\rho_{m}=\max_{i\in[n]}(\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}},\frac{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{R_{i}}^{2}}{\tr\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}^{2}})
for simplicity. In the following, we analyze the coefficient

c=\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor}\binom{n}{2k_{0}}((2\rho_{m})^{d}\eta^{(d-1)})^{2k_{0}}.

(420)

From Ref.
[
32
]
, coefficient
c
has an upper bound:

c=\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\lfloor n/4\rfloor}\binom{n}{2k_{0}}((2\rho_{m})^{d}\eta^{(d-1)})^{2k_{0}}\leq(\eta(2\rho_{m})^{\frac{d}{d-1}})^{d-d^{*}},

(421)

where

d^{*}=\frac{\log n}{\log\frac{1}{2\eta}}+\frac{\log(e-1)}{\log\frac{1}{2\eta}}+1.

(422)

Recall that we choose depth
d
such that
d-d^{*}=\log\frac{n}{\varepsilon}=O(\log n)
. In the large
n
limit,
\eta(2\rho_{m})^{\frac{d}{d-1}}\leq 2\eta\rho_{m}+\delta
with
\delta
an arbitrarily small number. Then,

c\leq(2\eta\rho_{m}+\delta)^{\log\frac{n}{\varepsilon}}=(\frac{\varepsilon}{n})^{\log\frac{1}{2\eta\rho_{m}+\delta}}.

(423)

As a result,

\tr O_{SE}\Phi^{2}_{\mathcal{L}_{n}^{d}}(O_{SR})\leq\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_E)\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_R)+\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{SE})\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{SR})+(\frac{\varepsilon}{n})^{\log\frac{1}{2\eta\rho_{m}+\delta}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}).

(424)

\mathbb{E}_{U}\|\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\rho_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})-\tau_{E}\otimes\rho_{R}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{SE})\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{SR})+(\frac{\varepsilon}{n})^{\log\frac{1}{2\eta\rho_{m}+\delta}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}(\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}}).

(425)

Note that
\widetilde{\rho}_{SR}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{2N}\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{S_{i}R_{i}}=\zeta_{R_{i}}^{-1/4}\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}\zeta_{R_{i}}^{-1/4}
. We can choose
\zeta_{R_{i}}=\rho_{R_{i}}
or
\zeta_{R}=\rho_{R}
to let

\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{R_i})=1,\tr(\widetilde{\rho}^2_{S_iR_i})=2^{-H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}}.

(426)

Thus,

\tr_{AR}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\rho}_{SR})^{2}=\tr_{AR}(\widetilde{\rho}_{AR}^{2})=2^{-H_{2}(A|R)_{\rho_{AR}}},

(427)

and

\rho_{m}=\max_{i\in[n]}(2^{H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}},2^{-H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}}).

(428)

Also, we can choose
\sigma_{E}=\tau_{E}
such that

\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{E})=1,\tr(\widetilde{\tau}^2_{SE})=2^{-H_{2}(S|E)_{\tau_{SE}}}.

(429)

In this case,

\tr_{AE}(\tr_{\bar{A}}\widetilde{\tau}_{SE})^{2}=\tr_{AE}(\widetilde{\tau}_{AE}^{2})=2^{-H_{2}(A|E)_{\tau_{AE}}}.

(430)

Then, for any
\delta>0
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{U}\|\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\rho_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})-\tau_{E}\otimes\rho_{R}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{2^{-H_{2}(S|E)_{\tau_{SE}}-H_{2}(S|R)_{\rho_{SR}}}+(\frac{\varepsilon}{n})^{\log\frac{1}{2\eta\rho_{m}+\delta}}\max_{A\subseteq[n]}2^{-H_{2}(A|R)_{\rho_{AR}}-H_{2}(A|E)_{\tau_{AE}}}}.

(431)

In the description of Theorem
2
, we omit
\delta
for simplicity. Proof is done.

C.3
Application of Theorem
2
in quantum error correction

Here, we present the results of applying Theorem
2
in quantum error correction and show that the encoding scheme induced by
\mathfrak{B}
can have a constant threshold against local noise.

For the quantum error correction task, there are
nR
physical qudits in total. We first fix the encoding rate
R=k/n
, where
R
is a constant. Then, we set the local dimension
q
as
2^{R^{-1}}
composed of
R^{-1}
qubits. The total qudit number is correspondingly changed from
n
to
nR
. Since
R
is a constant, the local dimension is still a constant. Then, each
S_{i}
is a qudit with local dimension
2^{R^{-1}}
, and
R_{i}
is a qubit. Without loss of generality, the state
\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}
is set as
\outerproduct{\hat{\phi}}{\hat{\phi}}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}^{R^{-1}-1}
where
\outerproduct{\hat{\phi}}{\hat{\phi}}
is an EPR pair. Then,
H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})=-1
,
\eta=\frac{2^{R^{-1}}}{2^{2R^{-1}}+1}
,
\rho_{m}=2
, and

\frac{1}{2\eta\rho_{m}}=\frac{2^{2R^{-1}}+1}{2^{R^{-1}+2}}=\frac{2^{2n/k}+1}{2^{n/k+2}}>2^{n/k-2}.

(432)

Meanwhile, we consider local noise. The channel
\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}
will be the complementary channel of local noise,
\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}=\hat{\mathcal{N}}^{\otimes n}
. In this case, the decoupling theorem can be simplified to

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\|\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\rho_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})-\tau_{E}\otimes\rho_{R}\|_{1}\\
\leq&\sqrt{2^{-\sum_{i=1}^{nR}H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}-\sum_{i=1}^{nR}H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}}+(\frac{\varepsilon}{nR})^{\log\frac{1}{2\eta\rho_{m}}}\prod_{i=1}^{nR}\max(1,2^{-H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}-H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}})},\end{split}

(433)

where
H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}=R^{-1}(1-f(\vec{p}))
with
f(\vec{p})=2\log(\sqrt{p_I} + \sqrt{p_X} + \sqrt{p_Y} + \sqrt{p_Z})
for strength-
\vec{p}
local Pauli noise,
H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}=R^{-1}(1-\log(1+3p))
for strength-
p
local erasure error, and
H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}=-R^{-1}\log( \frac{1}{2-p} + \sqrt{\frac{p}{2-p}} )
for strength-
p
local amplitude damping noise.

For local Pauli noise,

H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}+H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}=R^{-1}(1-f(\vec{p}))-1=R^{-1}(1-f(\vec{p})-\frac{k}{n}).

(434)

When
1-f(\vec{p})-\frac{k}{n}>0
, the term
\max(1,2^{-H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})_{\tau_{S_{i}E_{i}}}-H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})_{\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}}})\leq 1
. It is also true for local erasure error when
1-\log(1+3p)-\frac{k}{n}>0
and for local amplitude damping noise when
-\log( \frac{1}{2-p} + \sqrt{\frac{p}{2-p}} )-\frac{k}{n}>0
.

Note that the expected Choi error satisfies

\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\|\mathcal{T}_{S\rightarrow E}(U_{S}\rho_{SR}U_{S}^{\dagger})-\tau_{E}\otimes\rho_{R}\|_{1}}.

(435)

We obtain that the Choi error is upper-bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq\left(2^{-n(1-f(\vec{p})-\frac{k}{n})}+(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{\frac{n}{k}-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}

(436)

for strength-
\vec{p}
local Pauli noise, upper-bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq\left(2^{-n(1-\log(1+3p)-\frac{k}{n})}+(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{\frac{n}{k}-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}

(437)

for strength-
p
local erasure error, and upper-bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq\left(2^{-n(-\log( \frac{1}{2-p} + \sqrt{\frac{p}{2-p}} )-\frac{k}{n})}+(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{\frac{n}{k}-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}

(438)

for strength-
p
local amplitude damping noise.

To let the Choi error vanish, we need to ensure
\frac{n}{k}-2>0
. Thus, we show that there exists a threshold
\min(\frac{1}{2},1-f(\vec{p}))
for local Pauli noise, a threshold
\min(\frac{1}{2},1-\log(1+3p)))
for local erasure error, and a threshold
\min(\frac{1}{2},-\log( \frac{1}{2-p} + \sqrt{\frac{p}{2-p}} ))
for local amplitude damping noise. Moreover, by choosing appropriate parameters,
(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{\frac{n}{k}-2}
will polynomially decay with
n
.

For strength-
p
nearest neighbor
ZZ
-coupling noise, we apply the original Theorem
2
. Similar to the analysis in Appendix
B.9
, we obtain that

\displaystyle H_{2}(A|R)_{\rho_{AR}}

\displaystyle=-\absolutevalue{A}

(439)

\displaystyle H_{2}(A|E)_{\tau_{AE}}

\displaystyle\geq R^{-1}\absolutevalue{A}[1-2(1+R)\log(\sqrt{1-p}+\sqrt{p})].

(440)

Thus, when
H_{2}(A|R)+H_{2}(A|E)\geq 0
, or equivalently,
1-R-2(1+R)\log(\sqrt{1-p}+\sqrt{p})\geq 0
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{U_{S}\sim\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}\leq\left(2^{-n(1-\frac{k}{n}-2(1+\frac{k}{n})\log(\sqrt{1-p}+\sqrt{p}))}+(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{\frac{n}{k}-2}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

(441)

Hence, the achievable encoding rate for
\mathfrak{B}_{n}^{\varepsilon}
is
1-2(1+\frac{k}{n})\log(\sqrt{1-p}+\sqrt{p})
.

Note that the above analysis also applies to the case of local dimension
2
. In this case, the total number of qubits is
n
, and each state
\rho_{S_{i}R_{i}}
is defined as
\frac{k}{n}\outerproduct{\hat{\phi}}{\hat{\phi}}+(1-\frac{k}{n})\outerproduct{0}{0}
. We then have
H_{2}(S_{i}|R_{i})=-\frac{k}{n}
, while
H_{2}(S_{i}|E_{i})
depends on the noise parameter, typically taking the form
1-f(\vec{p})
for local Pauli noise. With
\eta=\frac{2}{5}
and
\rho_{m}=2^{\frac{k}{n}}
, substituting these values into the decoupling theorem yields the same bound, except that the factor
(\frac{\varepsilon}{k})^{\frac{n}{k}-2}
is replaced by
(\frac{\varepsilon}{n})^{\log\frac{5}{4}-\frac{k}{n}}
.

Appendix D
Lower bound for circuit depth in AQEC

Here, we analyze the lower bound on the circuit depth required for a unitary encoding of

k
logical qubits that achieves a Choi error
\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}
. For the
2k
-qubit maximally entangled state
\hat{\phi}_{LR}
shared between the reference system
R
and the logical system
L
, where both
L
and
R
consist of
k
qubits each, we denote the
i
-th qubit in
L
by
L_{i,0}
and the qubit in
R
that is maximally entangled with
L_{i,0}
by
R_{i,0}
. We define the physical system
S
as the union of
L
and the ancillary system initialized in the state
\ket{0^{n-k}}
.

Consider a unitary circuit
U
of depth
d
acting on
S
, decomposed as

U=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left(\bigotimes_{j}V_{i,j}\right),

(442)

where each
\{V_{i,j}\}_{j}
represents the set of two-qubit gates in the
i
-th layer of
U
with disjoint supports. For each layer
j
, where
1\leq j\leq d
, let
L_{i,j}
denote the union of the supports of all two-qubit gates that intersect with
L_{i,j-1}
:

L_{i,j}=\bigcup\left\{\mathrm{supp}(V_{i,j})\;\big|\;\mathrm{supp}(V_{i,j})\cap L_{i,j-1}\neq\emptyset\right\}.

(443)

In other words,
L_{i,j}
represents the light cone of the
i
-th qubit in the first
j
layers of
U
.

Similarly, we define the backward light cone of the
i
-th physical qubit,
i\in S
, recursively as

\begin{split}B_{i,d}&=\{i\},\\
B_{i,j}&=\bigcup\left\{\mathrm{supp}(V_{i,j+1})\;\big|\;\mathrm{supp}(V_{i,j+1})\cap B_{i,j+1}\neq\emptyset\right\},\quad 0\leq j<d.\end{split}

(444)

By the definitions of
L_{i,j}
and
B_{i,j}
, for any logical qubit
i\in L
and physical qubit
k\in S
, the qubit
k
is included in
L_{i,d}
if and only if the logical qubit
i
is included in
B_{k,0}
.

Finally, we define
M
as an upper bound on the size of all light cones:

M=\max\left(\max_{i\in L}|L_{i,d}|,\max_{i\in S}|B_{i,0}|\right).

(445)

With this definition, we can establish a lower bound on the number of physical qubits in
L
that have disjoint light cones, as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 14

(Disjoint light cones)
.

For any unitary circuit
U=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left(\bigotimes_{j}V_{i,j}\right)
, there exists a subset
J\subseteq L
such that:

1.

For any distinct qubits
i,j\in J
, their light cones are disjoint:
L_{i,d}\cap L_{j,d}=\emptyset
.

2.

The size of
J
satisfies
|J|\geq\left\lceil\frac{k}{M^{2}}\right\rceil
.

Proof.

We construct the set
J
iteratively. Start with an empty set
J_{0}=\emptyset
. At each step
a
, assume we have a set
J_{a}
with disjoint light cones. Define
L_{J_{a},d}=\bigcup_{i\in J_{a}}L_{i,d}
, the union of the light cones of qubits in
J_{a}
.

For a qubit
j\in L
, the condition that
L_{j,d}
does not intersect with any
L_{i,d}
for
i\in J_{a}
,

L_{j,d}\cap L_{J_{a},d}=\emptyset,

(446)

is equivalent to

j\notin\bigcup_{m\in L_{J_{a},d}}B_{m,0}.

(447)

Since each
L_{i,d}
has at most
M
qubits, the size of
L_{J_{a},d}
is at most
|J_{a}|M
. Consequently, the set
\bigcup_{m\in L_{J_{a},d}}B_{m,0}
has size at most
|J_{a}|M^{2}
.

If
k>|J_{a}|M^{2}
, there exists at least one logical qubit
i\in L-J_{a}
such that
i\notin\bigcup_{m\in L_{J_{a},d}}B_{m,0}
. We can then add this qubit to
J_{a}
to form
J_{a+1}=J_{a}\cup\{i\}
, maintaining the property that the light cones of qubits in
J_{a+1}
remain disjoint.

This process continues until
k\leq|J_{a}|M^{2}=aM^{2}
. Therefore, the final set
J_{a}
satisfies that the light cones of any two distinct qubits in
J
are disjoint, and
|J_{a}|=a\geq\frac{k}{M^{2}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.

We now combine Lemma
14
with the properties of depolarizing noise to analyze circuit lower bound. Recall that the Choi fidelity is defined as

F_{\mathrm{Choi}}=\max_{\mathcal{D}}F(\hat{\phi}_{LR},(\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{U})_{L}(\hat{\phi}_{LR})).

(448)

Here, and in the following, we omit the identity channel
I_{R}
on the reference system. The encoding channel
\mathcal{U}
maps a state
\rho
on system
L
to another state
\mathcal{U}(\rho)=U(\rho\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}})U^{\dagger}
on system
S
. The noise channel
\mathcal{N}
represents i.i.d. local depolarizing noise on system
S
, specifically
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{d}^{S}
, where
\mathcal{N}_{d}^{S}=\bigotimes_{i\in S}\mathcal{N}_{d}^{(i)}
and
\mathcal{N}_{d}^{(i)}
is the strength-
p
depolarizing channel acting on the
i
-th qubit. The recovery channel
\mathcal{D}
maps a state on system
S
back to a state on system
L
.

Consider the subset
J
obtained in Lemma
14
under the strength-
p
local depolarizing noise defined in Eq. (
36
). For a single qubit
i\in J
, the probability that all physical qubits in
L_{i,d}
are traced out and replaced by the maximally mixed state is
p_{L}=p^{|L_{i,d}|}\geq p^{M}
. Since the light cones of logical qubits in
J
are disjoint, the events that these light cones are traced out are independent. Therefore, the probability that none of these light cones are fully traced out is

p_{\mathrm{None}}=\prod_{i\in J}\left(1-p^{|L_{i,d}|}\right)\leq\left(1-p^{M}\right)^{|J|}\leq\exp\left(-|J|\cdot p^{M}\right).

(449)

The second inequality utilizes the bound
(1-x)\leq e^{-x}
for
0\leq x\leq 1
. This probability provides a lower bound for the Choi error, as later obtained in Eq. (
458
). To see this, consider the Choi fidelity under the local depolarizing channel:

F_{\mathrm{Choi}}=\max_{\mathcal{D}}F\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR},\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}\circ\mathcal{N}_{d}^{S}\left[U_{d}\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR}\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}}\right)U_{d}^{\dagger}\right]\right).

(450)

Expanding the state after passing through the noise channel
\mathcal{N}_{d}^{S}
, with probability
1-p_{\mathrm{None}}
, the noise erases all information in at least one of the
L_{i,d}
systems for
i\in J
, replacing it with a maximally mixed state. Consequently, the state after applying the local depolarizing noise is

\mathcal{N}_{d}^{S}\left[U_{d}\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR}\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}}\right)U_{d}^{\dagger}\right]=\sum_{l}p_{l}\psi_{l}+p_{\mathrm{None}}\psi^{\prime},

(451)

where
\sum_{l}p_{l}=1-p_{\mathrm{None}}
represents the probability that at least one logical qubit’s light cone is traced out. Here, the indices
l
are used to distinguish states subject to errors on different subsets of physical qubits.
For each
\psi_{l}
, at least one subsystem
L_{i,d}
with
i\in J
is replaced by the maximally mixed state.

The square of the Choi fidelity can then be expressed as

\begin{split}F^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}&=\max_{\mathcal{D}}F^{2}\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR},\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}\circ\mathcal{N}_{d}^{S}\left[U_{d}\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR}\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}}\right)U_{d}^{\dagger}\right]\right)\\
&=\max_{\mathcal{D}}F^{2}\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR},\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}\left[\sum_{l}p_{l}\psi_{l}+p_{\mathrm{None}}\psi^{\prime}\right]\right)\\
&=\max_{\mathcal{D}}\left\langle\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right|\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}\left[\sum_{l}p_{l}\psi_{l}+p_{\mathrm{None}}\psi^{\prime}\right]\left|\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right\rangle\\
&=\max_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\sum_{l}p_{l}\left\langle\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right|\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}(\psi_{l})\left|\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right\rangle+p_{\mathrm{None}}\left\langle\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right|\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}(\psi^{\prime})\left|\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right\rangle\right)\\
&\leq\max_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\sum_{l}p_{l}\left\langle\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right|\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}(\psi_{l})\left|\hat{\phi}_{LR}\right\rangle+p_{\mathrm{None}}\right).\end{split}

(452)

In the third equality, we utilize Eq. (
13
). The fourth equality follows from the linearity of quantum channels.

Now, consider the quantity
\langle\hat{\phi}_{LR}|\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}(\psi_{l})|\hat{\phi}_{LR}\rangle
. Since at least the light cone of one logical qubit in
L
is fully traced out, we have

\psi_{l}=\mathrm{Tr}_{T}\left[U\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR}\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}}\right)U^{\dagger}\right]\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{T}}{2^{|T|}},

(453)

for some subset
T\subseteq S
such that
L_{i,d}\subseteq T
for some
i\in L
. Crucially, when the light cone
L_{i,d}
is traced out, the unitaries applied within
L_{i,d}
are canceled. Therefore, we can replace the initial state
\hat{\phi}_{LR}
with
\hat{\phi}_{L-L_{i,0},R-R_{i,0}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{R_{i,0}}}{2}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{L_{i,0}}}{2}
. Specifically,

\tr_{L_{i,d}}\left[U\left(\hat{\phi}_{LR}\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}}\right)U^{\dagger}\right]=\tr_{L_{i,d}}\left[U\left(\hat{\phi}_{L-L_{i,0},R-R_{i,0}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{L_{i,0}}}{2}\otimes\outerproduct{0^{n-k}}{0^{n-k}}\right)U^{\dagger}\right]\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{R_{i,0}}}{2}.

(454)

Thus, the state
\psi_{l}
in Eq. (
452
) can be written as

\begin{split}\psi_{l}=\hat{\psi}_{l}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{T}}{2^{|T|}}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{R_{0}}}{2}.\end{split}

(455)

where
\hat{\psi}_{l}=\tr_{T\cup R_{i,0}}(\psi_{l})
. We then have

\bra{\hat{\phi}_{LR}}\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}\left(\hat{\psi}_{l}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{T}}{2^{|T|}}\right)\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{R_{0}}}{2}\ket{\hat{\phi}_{LR}}=\max_{\mathcal{D}}\frac{1}{4}\bra{\hat{\phi}_{L-L_{i,0},R-R_{i,0}}}\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}\left(\hat{\psi}\otimes\frac{\mathbb{I}_{T}}{2^{|T|}}\right)\ket{\hat{\phi}_{L-L_{i,0},R-R_{i,0}}}\leq\frac{1}{4}.

(456)

Therefore, the Choi fidelity in Eq. (
452
) satisfies

\begin{split}F^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}&\leq\max_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\sum_{l}p_{l}\langle\hat{\phi}_{LR}|\mathcal{D}_{S\rightarrow L}(\psi_{l})|\hat{\phi}_{LR}\rangle+p_{\mathrm{None}}\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{l}\frac{p_{l}}{4}+p_{\mathrm{None}}=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3}{4}p_{\mathrm{None}}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3}{4}\exp\left(-|J|\cdot p^{M}\right),\end{split}

(457)

where the last inequality follows from Eq. (
449
). Recall that
1-\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}^{2}=F^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}
. For
\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}<0.1
, we have
\exp\left(-|J|\cdot p^{M}\right)>0.98
. In this regime, using the inequality
e^{-x}\leq 1-\frac{x}{2}
, we obtain

\begin{split}F^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}&\leq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3}{4}\left(1-\frac{|J|}{2}p^{M}\right)=1-\frac{3|J|}{8}p^{M},\\
\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}&\geq\frac{3|J|}{8}p^{M}.\end{split}

(458)

Taking the logarithm of both sides, we obtain

M\log\left(\frac{1}{p}\right)-\log(|J|)\geq\log\left(\frac{3}{8\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}}\right).

(459)

According to Lemma
14
,
|J|\geq\frac{k}{M^{2}}
. Therefore, we have

M\log\left(\frac{1}{p}\right)+2\log M\geq\log\left(\frac{3}{8\epsilon^{2}_{\mathrm{Choi}}}\right)+\log k.

(460)

For a
D
-dimensional quantum circuit, the size of the light cones can be upper bounded by
M\leq(2d)^{D}
, since the length of each edge of the light-cone hypercube is upper-bounded by
2d
. Substituting this bound into the previous inequality, we obtain

(2d)^{D}\log\left(\frac{1}{p}\right)+2D\log(2d)\geq\log\left(\frac{3}{8\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}^{2}}\right)+\log k.

(461)

In the case of an all-to-all circuit, the size of the light cones is upper bounded by
M\leq 2^{d}
. Therefore, the inequality becomes

2^{d}\log\left(\frac{1}{p}\right)+2d\geq\log\left(\frac{3}{8\epsilon_{\mathrm{Choi}}^{2}}\right)+\log k.

(462)

∎
